Under arithmetic mean $(\\tof{+}\\nof)/2$, qualitative rankings are preserved but collapsed methods (0.498--0.500) appear indistinguishable from vanilla (0.509), masking behavioral death; harmonic mean's zero-floor on $\\tof{=}0$ is by design.}\n\\label{tab:main_comparison}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & Type & $\\sycon$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\tof$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\nof$ $\\uparrow$ & Step \\\\\n\\midrule\nVanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO with $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\nCosine $\\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & 0.000$^\\S$ & 0.000 & 1.000 & 500 \\\\\nContinuous Probe $\\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{SFT with $\\dsyc$ probe penalty}} \\\\\nBinary Probe $\\rone$ (F2) & SFT & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO without $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.153$^\\dagger$ & 0.083 & 0.983 & 6500 \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Reference methods (not directly comparable)$^\\P$}} \\\\\nActAdd ($\\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\\\\n\\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \\textbf{0.301} & \\textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\\\\nACT (seed 2)$^\\ddagger$ & SFT & 0.286 & -- & -- & 7000 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\nIf the cosine and probe failures (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}) were caused by $\\dsyc$ alone, removing it should recover calibration.\n$\\rthree$-only GRPO tests this hypothesis by relying solely on the NLI behavioral reward---and fails: only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla (14.3\\%), with both seeds oscillating ($\\Delta \\approx 0.069$) before diverging (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}; Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\nThe failure is not representational: $\\dsyc$ AUROC varies by ${<}0.01$ across checkpoints and $\\rthree$ saturates at ${\\sim}0.98$ ($p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ swings by 17\\%.\nThis disconnect between stable representations and unstable behavior points to the per-completion reward structure, not the reward content, as the bottleneck.\n\n\\paragraph{Competing hypotheses and ablation.}\nThree explanations are consistent with these failures: (a)~$\\dsyc$ is fundamentally unsuitable as a sycophancy reward; (b)~group size$=$2 provides insufficient ranking diversity; (c)~sycophancy calibration resists scalar per-completion RL.\nWe designed two ablations to discriminate among them (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}).\n\\emph{Group-size ablation.}\nPer-completion GRPO fails at both group sizes 2 and 8; the gs$=$8 trajectory ($0.084 \\to 0.085 \\to 0.054$, all below vanilla; terminated early) rules out insufficient ranking diversity, though whether substantially larger groups (16--64) or alternative RL algorithms would succeed remains open.\n\\emph{LoRA-controlled comparison.}\nReducing ACT to GRPO's LoRA configuration (rank 64, $\\alpha{=}64$, 4 modules) still yields $\\sycon = 0.267$ (peak 0.326), with all 16 checkpoints exceeding vanilla.\nUnder identical adapters, per-token SFT exceeds per-completion GRPO by $1.7{\\times}$ ($0.267$ vs.\\ $0.153$), identifying supervision granularity as one plausible explanation, pending further isolation---independently corroborated by SMART's per-step success~\\citep{beigi2025smart}---though sequence length (256 vs.\\ 1024), data source (online vs.\\ curated), and dropout (0 vs.\\ 0.05) remain acknowledged confounds.\nThe probe reward trap provides independent evidence for~(a): it arises under SFT, where group size is irrelevant.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Ablation: isolating supervision paradigm from LoRA configuration. All methods use Qwen3-8B with $\\rthree$ (behavioral reward). Vanilla $\\sycon{=}0.134$. $^\\dagger$Terminated early after three checkpoints showed declining trajectory. $^\\ddagger$Peak at step 7000; final checkpoint (step 8000) $\\sycon{=}0.267$.}\n\\label{tab:ablation}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n\\toprule\nMethod & LoRA config & Best $\\sycon$ & ${>}$Van. \\\\\n\\midrule\nGRPO gs$=$2 (2 seeds) & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.153 & 4/28 \\\\\nGRPO gs$=$8$^\\dagger$ & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.085 & 0/3 \\\\\n\\midrule\nACT reduced-LoRA$^\\ddagger$ & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.326 & 16/16 \\\\\nACT full s1 & r64, $\\alpha$128, 7m & 0.301 & 16/16 \\\\\nACT full s2 & r64, $\\alpha$128, 7m & 0.286 & 15/15 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\sycon$ trajectories for $\\rthree$-only GRPO (two seeds) and ACT (two seeds) over training steps. ACT exceeds vanilla at every evaluated checkpoint (31/31 across two seeds); $\\rthree$-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/28 checkpoints (14.3\\%).}\n\\label{fig:trajectory}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{ACT vs.\\ GRPO.}\nACT's advantage is not marginal but categorical: its worst checkpoint ($\\sycon{=}0.170$) exceeds GRPO's absolute best ($\\sycon{=}0.153$), and both seeds maintain above-vanilla performance throughout training (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}; held-out validation confirms generalization, see Limitations).\nThe reduced-LoRA ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}) shows this is not an adapter-capacity artifact: reduced-LoRA ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.326$) exceeds full ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.301$), leaving supervision granularity---per-token vs.\\ per-completion---as one plausible explanatory variable, pending further isolation.\n\n\\paragraph{Relationship to inference-time probe methods.}\n\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking without producing gradients.\nThis suggests two orthogonal failure dimensions: (1)~inside vs.\\ outside the optimization loop---gradient exploitation enables the probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}), while inference-time reranking sidesteps it; and (2)~scalar per-completion reward---the failure persists even without the probe ($\\rthree$-only GRPO).\n\n\\paragraph{Future directions.}\nPer-step process rewards offer the most direct path forward.\nSMART~\\citep{beigi2025smart} succeeds at sycophancy calibration by evaluating behavioral transitions at individual reasoning steps via MCTS.\nReasoning trajectories provide temporal structure---which step introduces evidence, which abandons a position---that a scalar per-completion reward compresses into a single number, losing the token-level information needed to distinguish valid revision from capitulation.\nMore broadly, condition-aware rewards, hybrid SFT-then-RL pipelines, and methods that incorporate evidential warrant deserve investigation; whether other REINFORCE-style methods or behavioral attributes with similar geometric entanglement~\\citep{skalse2022hacking} exhibit the same failures remains open.\n",
```json
      "numLines": 104,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 104
    }
  },
```

```json
          " SMART~\\citep{beigi2025smart} succeeds at sycophancy calibration by evaluating behavioral transitions at individual reasoning steps via MCTS.",
```

```json
          "-The reduced-LoRA ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}) shows this is not an adapter-capacity artifact: reduced-LoRA ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.326$) exceeds full ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.301$), leaving supervision granularity---per-token vs.\\ per-completion---as one plausible explanatory variable, pending further isolation.",
          "+The reduced-LoRA ablation (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}) shows this is not an adapter-capacity artifact: reduced-LoRA ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.326$) exceeds full ACT ($\\sycon{=}0.301$), leaving supervision granularity, specifically per-token vs.\\ per-completion, as one plausible explanatory variable, pending further isolation.",

          " \\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy using a probe of comparable quality (94\\% accuracy) via Best-of-N reranking without producing gradients."
```

```json
    "snippet": "13\tSycophancy is pervasive in RLHF-trained assistants \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, amplified by RLHF \\citep{shapira2026amplifies}, and traceable to late-layer preference shifts \\citep{wang2025internal}; mitigations include synthetic data \\citep{wei2024synthetic}, consistency training \\citep{consistency2025bctact}, and neuron-level correction \\citep{obrien2025fewbadneurons}.\n14\tMore fundamentally, \\citet{vennemeyer2025sycophancy} demonstrate that sycophancy decomposes into distinct, independently steerable sub-behaviors, each encoded along separate directions; a single $\\dsyc$ captures only their shared variance.\n15\tOur analysis shows why the step from diagnosis to reward is structurally blocked: $\\dsyc$ conflates warranted revision with unwarranted capitulation.\n16\t\n17\t\\paragraph{Probe-Based and Representation-Guided Methods.}\n18\t\\citet{papadatos2024probepenalties} reduce sycophancy via Best-of-N reranking with linear probe penalties (94\\% accuracy) without producing gradients; our work reveals what happens when a comparable signal enters the training loop (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n19\tLatent-GRPO \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, CRAFT \\citep{luo2026craft}, \\citet{sinii2025smallvectors}, and \\citet{ackermann2026gradreg} extend representation-guided RL to reasoning and gradient regularization.\n20\tSMART \\citep{beigi2025smart} applies per-step process rewards for sycophancy, succeeding where our completion-level rewards fail, consistent with ACT's per-token success."
  },
```

### Tool result

_source line 194 · 2026-05-24T13:00:34.963Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JUy2UxsWC1MX3D29Q2Zyyg`</summary>

```text
1	We quantify a temporal decoupling we call the \emph{probe reward trap}: when a frozen linear probe on a contrastive sycophancy direction ($\dsyc$, cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B, $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B) is used as an SFT training signal, behavioral death (true-positive flip rate $= 0$) occurs within 500 steps while generation entropy remains healthy (0.70). In our experiments, the resulting 1{,}300-step lag before distributional collapse means that entropy monitoring, the most common safeguard against reward hacking, misses the failure entirely. Because the trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, it is independent of RL optimization dynamics, revealing a semantic limitation of the direction itself: $\dsyc$ encodes \emph{whether} belief changed, not \emph{whether it should have}. The failure extends beyond probe rewards: all $\dsyc$-based GRPO formulations collapse, and even behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/31 checkpoints, while dense per-token SFT succeeds at every checkpoint (47/47). A LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible explanation, pending further isolation, with sequence length, data source, and dropout as acknowledged confounds, suggesting that per-completion scalar rewards may compress away the token-level information needed for sycophancy calibration.
2	
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	The probe reward trap reveals that behavioral death can precede distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy-based monitoring blind to the failure.
4	Because the trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, it reflects a semantic limitation of $\dsyc$, not an RL artifact; under GRPO, all formulations failed (4/31 checkpoints ${>}$ vanilla across configurations) while per-token SFT succeeded at every checkpoint (47/47), and a LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible factor, pending further isolation~(\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
5	Our results suggest that, at least for sycophancy, discriminability alone does not suffice for reward utility; similar failure modes may arise for other attributes where the target behavior and its complement overlap geometrically, and condition-aware rewards with dense supervision offer the most promising path forward.
6	
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01TfsGc9Gtmjg4U52Ao6kn2p`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	\begin{figure*}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_teaser.pdf}
7	\caption{The probe reward trap. (a)~$\dsyc$ discriminates sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations (AUROC $0.827$), but valid revision and unwarranted capitulation overlap along the same direction. (b)~When used as an SFT reward, behavioral death ($\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500 while entropy remains healthy; the 1{,}300-step lag renders standard monitoring blind.}
8	\label{fig:teaser}
9	\end{figure*}
10	
11	Representation engineering \citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.
12	A contrastive sycophancy direction $\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\S\ref{sec:direction_quality}), though 256-token truncation during GRPO training degrades this to approximately 0.60 (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).
13	These results point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a training signal.
14	Sycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level signal could target the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.
15	
16	This paper documents the \emph{probe reward trap}: when a frozen linear probe on $\dsyc$ is used as an SFT training signal with stopped gradients, behavioral death ($\tof = 0$) occurs within 500 steps while generation entropy remains healthy (0.70), masking the failure from standard monitoring (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}).
17	In our experiments, the 1{,}300-step lag before distributional collapse means entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking \citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout.
18	Because the trap arises under SFT rather than GRPO, it reflects a limitation of $\dsyc$ itself: the direction encodes \emph{whether} belief changed, not \emph{whether it should have}, so any reward conditioned on it alone will either be gamed or reward rigidity.
19	
20	The failure is not specific to probe rewards.
21	Under our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), cosine projection and continuous probe formulations of $\dsyc$ also collapse, and removing $\dsyc$ entirely does not help: behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 31 checkpoints (12.9\%).
22	By contrast, ACT \citep{consistency2025bctact}, which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion, exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
23	A LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible explanation for this observed reliability gap, pending further isolation.
24	
25	The root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.
26	Techniques for discovering latent knowledge \citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability should transfer into a useful training signal.
27	For reasoning faithfulness and safety directions, this premise holds because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve.
28	Sycophancy violates this condition: capitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\dsyc$ \citep{wang2025internal, shapira2026amplifies}.
29	When reward overoptimization \citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \citep{skalse2022hacking}.
30	
31	This paper presents a negative result with systematic diagnostic analysis.
32	Our two principal findings (Figure~\ref{fig:teaser}):
33	
34	\begin{itemize}
35	\item \textbf{The probe reward trap} (C2): behavioral death ($\tof{=}0$) precedes distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy monitoring blind. The trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, implicating $\dsyc$ itself rather than the optimizer (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}).
36	
37	\item \textbf{Observed reliability gap between supervision paradigms} (C1): per-token SFT exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47 across all configurations and seeds) while per-completion GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/31 (12.9\%); under matched LoRA, the magnitude gap is $1.7{\times}$ (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
38	\end{itemize}
39	
```

Because the trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, it is independent of RL optimization dynamics, revealing a semantic limitation of the direction itself: $\\dsyc$ encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}. The failure extends beyond probe rewards: all $\\dsyc$-based GRPO formulations collapse, and even behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/31 checkpoints, while dense per-token SFT succeeds at every checkpoint (47/47). A LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible explanation, pending further isolation, with sequence length, data source, and dropout as acknowledged confounds, suggesting that per-completion scalar rewards may compress away the token-level information needed for sycophancy calibration.\n",

```json
    "newString": "(4/28 checkpoints ${>}$ vanilla across two seeds; gs$=$8 ablation: 0/3)",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThe probe reward trap reveals that behavioral death can precede distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy-based monitoring blind to the failure.\nBecause the trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, it reflects a semantic limitation of $\\dsyc$, not an RL artifact; under GRPO, all formulations failed (4/31 checkpoints ${>}$ vanilla across configurations) while per-token SFT succeeded at every checkpoint (47/47), and a LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible factor, pending further isolation~(\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nOur results suggest that, at least for sycophancy, discriminability alone does not suffice for reward utility; similar failure modes may arise for other attributes where the target behavior and its complement overlap geometrically, and condition-aware rewards with dense supervision offer the most promising path forward.\n",
```

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "1f9e9764-2179-4ef0-ab99-07737e02d61a",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\n\\begin{figure*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\textwidth]{figures/fig_teaser.pdf}\n\\caption{The probe reward trap. (a)~$\\dsyc$ discriminates sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations (AUROC $0.827$), but valid revision and unwarranted capitulation overlap along the same direction. (b)~When used as an SFT reward, behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500 while entropy remains healthy; the 1{,}300-step lag renders standard monitoring blind.}\n\\label{fig:teaser}\n\\end{figure*}\n\nRepresentation engineering \\citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.\nA contrastive sycophancy direction $\\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\\S\\ref{sec:direction_quality}), though 256-token truncation during GRPO training degrades this to approximately 0.60 (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).\nThese results point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a training signal.\nSycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level signal could target the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.\n\nThis paper documents the \\emph{probe reward trap}: when a frozen linear probe on $\\dsyc$ is used as an SFT training signal with stopped gradients, behavioral death ($\\tof = 0$) occurs within 500 steps while generation entropy remains healthy (0.70), masking the failure from standard monitoring (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\nIn our experiments, the 1{,}300-step lag before distributional collapse means entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking \\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout.\nBecause the trap arises under SFT rather than GRPO, it reflects a limitation of $\\dsyc$ itself: the direction encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}, so any reward conditioned on it alone will either be gamed or reward rigidity.\n\nThe failure is not specific to probe rewards.\nUnder our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), cosine projection and continuous probe formulations of $\\dsyc$ also collapse, and removing $\\dsyc$ entirely does not help: behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 31 checkpoints (12.9\\%).\nBy contrast, ACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact}, which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion, exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nA LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible explanation for this observed reliability gap, pending further isolation.\n\nThe root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.\nTechniques for discovering latent knowledge \\citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability should transfer into a useful training signal.\nFor reasoning faithfulness and safety directions, this premise holds because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve.\nSycophancy violates this condition: capitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\\dsyc$ \\citep{wang2025internal, shapira2026amplifies}.\nWhen reward overoptimization \\citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n\nThis paper presents a negative result with systematic diagnostic analysis.\nOur two principal findings (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}):\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{The probe reward trap} (C2): behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) precedes distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy monitoring blind. The trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, implicating $\\dsyc$ itself rather than the optimizer (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Observed reliability gap between supervision paradigms} (C1): per-token SFT exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47 across all configurations and seeds) while per-completion GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/31 (12.9\\%); under matched LoRA, the magnitude gap is $1.7{\\times}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [

        "newStart": 18,

          " ",
          " The failure is not specific to probe rewards.",
          "-Under our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), cosine projection and continuous probe formulations of $\\dsyc$ also collapse, and removing $\\dsyc$ entirely does not help: behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 31 checkpoints (12.9\\%).",
          "+Under our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), cosine projection and continuous probe formulations of $\\dsyc$ also collapse, and removing $\\dsyc$ entirely does not help: behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 28 checkpoints across two seeds (14.3\\%).",
          " By contrast, ACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact}, which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion, exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).",
```

```json
{
  "parentUuid": "c602bf94-ae8e-406e-8ff6-b19ffb2fccc8",

    "snippet": "1\t\\section{When Does Discriminability Fail as Reward Utility?}\\label{sec:discussion}\n2\t\n3\t\\paragraph{Root cause of the probe reward trap.}\n4\tThe probe reward trap (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}) and the cosine failures (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}) share a common geometric root.\n5\t$\\dsyc$ encodes the \\emph{direction} of belief change but not its \\emph{evidential warrant}: valid revision and sycophantic capitulation both shift activations toward the user's position along the same direction.\n6\tThe two reward families expose complementary faces of this blindness.\n7\tCosine $\\rone$ penalizes all belief change indiscriminately; the resulting mode collapse (F1c, F1d) reflects excessive punishment of any activation shift along $\\dsyc$.\n8\tThe binary probe $\\rone$ rewards all resistance regardless of input validity; the stubbornness trap ($\\tof = 0$ by step 500) reflects excessive permissiveness toward activation stasis.\n9\tThese are not independent design flaws but dual consequences of a direction that conflates the target behavior (resisting invalid challenges) with its complement (refusing valid corrections).\n10\tCross-model evidence confirms the limitation extends beyond Qwen3-8B: Llama-3-8B independently achieves AUROC $0.779 \\pm 0.019$ on the same contrastive pairs with the same monotonic evidence gradient, so discriminability generalizes across architectures while the semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility persists.\n11\tThis pattern contrasts with reasoning faithfulness and safety directions~\\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo}, where the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve along the relevant direction.\n12\t\\citet{wu2026rewardhacking} reinforce this principle: concept directions applied via advantage modification in GRPO achieve 100\\% validation accuracy for reward hacking detection, where shortcut behaviors are geometrically separable.\n13\tA quantitative comparison of geometric separability between $\\dsyc$ and their reward-hacking directions remains an important open question.\n14\tIndependently, \\citet{kelkar2026persona} find that persona vectors are geometrically independent of $\\dsyc$ in activation space, further suggesting that $\\dsyc$ does not encode the full semantic structure of sycophancy.\n15\tThe multi-dimensional decomposition of sycophancy into independently steerable sub-behaviors~\\citep{vennemeyer2025sycophancy} compounds this limitation: $\\dsyc$ projects distinct sub-phenomena onto a single axis, amplifying the warranted-vs-unwarranted ambiguity.\n16\tFor sycophancy, the target and its complement occupy the same activation region; they differ only in whether external evidence warrants the belief change.\n17\t\n18\t\\paragraph{GRPO results under our configuration.}\n19\t\n20\t\\begin{table}[t]\n21\t\\centering\n22\t\\caption{Comparison of all sycophancy calibration methods on Qwen3-8B. $\\sycon$ is the harmonic mean of $\\tof$ and $\\nof$; higher indicates better calibration. Best result in \\textbf{bold}. $^\\dagger$Best checkpoint (seed 1, step 6500); only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla. $^\\ddagger$Seed 2 (15 checkpoints); all exceed vanilla. Best $\\sycon{=}0.286$ (step 7000). $^\\S$All cosine variants collapsed from step 1 ($\\tof{=}0$ at every checkpoint); F1d additionally diverged at step 2160 (entropy ${<}\\,0.01$). $^\\P$ACT and ActAdd differ from GRPO along 5+ dimensions (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}); included for context, not as controlled comparisons. Under arithmetic mean $(\\tof{+}\\nof)/2$, qualitative rankings are preserved but collapsed methods (0.498--0.500) appear indistinguishable from vanilla (0.509), masking behavioral death; harmonic mean's zero-floor on $\\tof{=}0$ is by design.}\n23\t\\label{tab:main_comparison}\n24\t\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n25\t\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcccc@{}}\n26\t\\toprule\n27\tMethod & Type & $\\sycon$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\tof$ $\\uparrow$ & $\\nof$ $\\uparrow$ & Step \\\\\n28\t\\midrule\n29\tVanilla & -- & 0.134 & 0.072 & 0.945 & -- \\\\\n30\t\\midrule\n31\t\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO with $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n32\tCosine $\\rone$ (F1a--F1d) & RL & 0.000$^\\S$ & 0.000 & 1.000 & 500 \\\\\n33\tContinuous Probe $\\rone$ (F3) & RL & 0.124 & 0.066 & 0.934 & 500 \\\\\n34\t\\midrule\n35\t\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{SFT with $\\dsyc$ probe penalty}} \\\\\n36\tBinary Probe $\\rone$ (F2) & SFT & 0.000 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 500 \\\\\n37\t\\midrule\n38\t\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{GRPO without $\\dsyc$}} \\\\\n39\t$\\rthree$-only GRPO & RL & 0.153$^\\dagger$ & 0.083 & 0.983 & 6500 \\\\\n40\t\\midrule\n41\t\\multicolumn{6}{l}{\\textit{Reference methods (not directly comparable)$^\\P$}} \\\\\n42\tActAdd ($\\alpha{=}5$) & Inference & 0.054 & 0.028 & 0.961 & -- \\\\\n43\t\\textbf{ACT} (seed 1) & SFT & \\textbf{0.301} & \\textbf{0.182} & 0.867 & 8000 \\\\\n44\tACT (seed 2)$^\\ddagger$ & SFT & 0.286 & -- & -- & 7000 \\\\\n45\t\\bottomrule\n46\t\\end{tabular}}\n47\t\\end{table}\n48\t\n49\tIf the cosine and probe failures (\\S\\ref{sec:cosine_failure}--\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}) were caused by $\\dsyc$ alone, removing it should recover calibration.\n50\t$\\rthree$-only GRPO tests this hypothesis by relying solely on the NLI behavioral reward, and fails: only 4/28 checkpoints across two seeds exceed vanilla (14.3\\%), with both seeds oscillating ($\\Delta \\approx 0.069$) before diverging (Table~\\ref{tab:main_comparison}; Figure~\\ref{fig:trajectory}).\n51\tThe failure is not representational: $\\dsyc$ AUROC varies by ${<}0.01$ across checkpoints and $\\rthree$ saturates at ${\\sim}0.98$ ($p{=}0.703$), yet $\\sycon$ swings by 17\\%.\n52\tThis disconnect between stable representations and unstable behavior points to the per-completion reward structure, not the reward content, as the bottleneck.\n53\t\n54\t\\paragraph{Competing hypotheses and ablation.}\n55\tThree explanations are consistent with these failures: (a)~$\\dsyc$ is fundamentally unsuitable as a sycophancy reward; (b)~group size$=$2 provides insufficient ranking diversity; (c)~sycophancy calibration resists scalar per-completion RL.\n56\tWe designed two ablations to discriminate among them (Table~\\ref{tab:ablation}).\n57\t\\emph{Group-size ablation.}\n58\tPer-completion GRPO fails at both group sizes 2 and 8; the gs$=$8 trajectory ($0.084 \\to 0.085 \\to 0.054$, all below vanilla; terminated early) rules out insufficient ranking diversity, though whether substantially larger groups (16--64) or alternative RL algorithms would succeed remains open.\n59\t\\emph{LoRA-controlled comparison.}\n60\tReducing ACT to GRPO's LoRA configuration (rank 64, $\\alpha{=}64$, 4 modules) still yields $\\sycon = 0.267$ (peak 0.326), with all 16 checkpoints exceeding vanilla.\n61\tUnder identical adapters, per-token SFT exceeds per-completion GRPO by $1.7{\\times}$ ($0.267$ vs.\\ $0.153$), identifying supervision granularity as one plausible explanation, pending further isolation, independently corroborated by SMART's per-step success~\\citep{beigi2025smart}, though sequence length (256 vs.\\ 1024), data source (online vs.\\ curated), and dropout (0 vs.\\ 0.05) remain acknowledged confounds.\n62\tThe probe reward trap provides independent evidence for~(a): it arises under SFT, where group size is irrelevant.\n63\t\n64\t\\begin{table}[t]\n65\t\\centering\n66\t\\small\n67\t\\caption{Ablation: isolating supervision paradigm from LoRA configuration. All methods use Qwen3-8B with $\\rthree$ (behavioral reward). Vanilla $\\sycon{=}0.134$. $^\\dagger$Terminated early after three checkpoints showed declining trajectory. $^\\ddagger$Peak at step 7000; final checkpoint (step 8000) $\\sycon{=}0.267$.}\n68\t\\label{tab:ablation}\n69\t\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n70\t\\begin{tabular}{@{}llcc@{}}\n71\t\\toprule\n72\tMethod & LoRA config & Best $\\sycon$ & ${>}$Van. \\\\\n73\t\\midrule\n74\tGRPO gs$=$2 (2 seeds) & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.153 & 4/28 \\\\\n75\tGRPO gs$=$8$^\\dagger$ & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.085 & 0/3 \\\\\n76\t\\midrule\n77\tACT reduced-LoRA$^\\ddagger$ & r64, $\\alpha$64, 4m & 0.326 & 16/16 \\\\\n78\tACT full s1 & r64, $\\alpha$128, 7m & 0.301 & 16/16 \\\\\n79\tACT full s2 & r64, $\\alpha$128, 7m & 0.286 & 15/15 \\\\\n80\t\\bottomrule\n81\t\\end{tabular}}\n82\t\\end{table}\n83\t\n84\t\\begin{figure}[t]\n85\t\\centering\n86\t\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_trajectory.pdf}\n\n... [18 lines truncated] ..."
```

```text
1	\section{The Probe Reward Trap}
2	\label{sec:probe_trap}
3	
4	The cosine formulations fail on signal magnitude; a natural response is to discretize.
5	Training a logistic probe on $\dsyc$ and thresholding its output yields $\rone = \mathbb{I}[P(\text{syc} \mid \mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \S\ref{ssec:reward}).
6	We show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.
7	Both probe experiments use SFT rather than GRPO (\S\ref{ssec:reward}); the trap is therefore independent of GRPO's optimization dynamics.
8	
9	\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}
10	Without stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy discovers within ${\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\tof = 0.978$, $\nof = 0.006$).
11	This motivates the frozen-probe design below.
12	
13	\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}
14	With stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.
15	By step 500, $\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\sycon = 0$.
16	Qualitative analysis of 181 valid-correction responses reveals five failure modes: \emph{deliberation traps} (28.7\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; \emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \emph{acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\%); \emph{genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\%); and refute/ignore/other (4.5\%). Full taxonomy in Appendix~\ref{app:taxonomy}.
17	
18	\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}
19	
20	\begin{table}[t]
21	\centering
22	\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}
23	\label{tab:probe_trajectory}
24	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
25	\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
26	\toprule
27	Step & $\tof$ & $\nof$ & $\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\
28	\midrule
29	500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\
30	1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\
31	1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\
32	2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\
33	\bottomrule
34	\end{tabular}}
35	\end{table}
36	
37	\begin{figure}[t]
38	\centering
39	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}
40	\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}
41	\label{fig:probe_trap}
42	\end{figure}
43	
44	Table~\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.
45	At step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000, well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy, and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\rone$ and $\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.
46	Entropy does not collapse until step ${\sim}1840$.
47	This gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.
48	Detecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.
49	
50	\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}
51	The differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.
52	With gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\dsyc$.
53	Any reward conditioned only on $\dsyc$ (continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen) will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.
54	Escaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\dsyc$ does not encode~\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.
55	
```

```text
1	\section{Training Details}
2	\label{app:training}
3	
4	\paragraph{Reward weight selection.}
5	The weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration: $w_1{=}0.25$ was initially chosen to down-weight the noisy cosine reward term; this was later increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ for F1d after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed the cosine term to contribute only ${\sim}8.3\%$ of total reward variance.
6	The empirical scaling factor in F1a ($\dsyc/20$) is a heuristic normalization; we did not find it critical to the failure modes reported.
7	
8	\paragraph{Response truncation.}
9	All GRPO experiments use \texttt{max\_completion\_length}=256, resulting in 80--84\% truncation of multi-turn responses.
10	This setting is shared across all conditions and does not affect relative comparisons; we retain it because response-level decisions typically manifest within the first 100--200 tokens.
11	
12	\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}
13	Table~\ref{tab:hyperparams} compares training configurations for GRPO variants and ACT.
14	
15	\begin{table}[h]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\caption{Training hyperparameters. All experiments use Qwen3-8B, DeepSpeed ZeRO-2, bf16 precision.}
19	\label{tab:hyperparams}
20	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
21	\toprule
22	& GRPO (F1, F3)$^e$ & ACT \\
23	\midrule
24	Learning rate & 5e-5 & 5e-5 \\
25	Effective batch & 16 & 16 \\
26	Max steps & 8{,}000 & 8{,}000 \\
27	Warmup steps & 200 & 200 \\
28	LR schedule & linear$^a$ & linear \\
29	Grad.\ clipping & --- & 1.0 \\
30	\midrule
31	LoRA rank & 64 & 64 \\
32	LoRA $\alpha$ & 64 & 128 \\
33	LoRA modules & 4$^b$ & 7$^c$ \\
34	LoRA dropout & 0 & 0.05 \\
35	\midrule
36	Group size & 2$^d$ & --- \\
37	KL $\beta$ & 0.04 & --- \\
38	Max compl.\ len. & 256 & --- \\
39	Max seq.\ len. & --- & 1{,}024 \\
40	L2 loss weight & --- & 0.1 \\
41	\bottomrule
42	\end{tabular}
43	\\[2pt]
44	\raggedright\footnotesize
45	$^a$Cosine schedule for $\rthree$-only.
46	$^b$q/k/v/o\_proj.
47	$^c$+ gate/up/down\_proj (${\sim}1.75{\times}$ params; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
48	$^d$Below typical 8--64 \citep{shao2024grpo}; not tuned.
49	$^e$Binary probe (F2) uses a custom SFT training loop rather than GRPO; group size is N/A. LoRA and optimizer settings match the GRPO column.
50	\end{table}
51	
52	\paragraph{GRPO reward variants.}
53	Cosine $\rone$ (F1d): $w_1{=}0.40$, scale$=$5.0, $w_3{=}0.60$; reward computed at layers [19, 20, 24, 27, 32, 33, 35]; recalibration every 500 steps (\S\ref{ssec:recalib}).
54	$\rthree$-only: $w_3{=}1.0$; NLI model \texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}.
55	F1a--F1c weight configurations are reported in Table~\ref{tab:cosine_chain}.
56	
57	\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}
58	\label{app:taxonomy}
59	
60	Manual inspection of all 181 valid-correction responses from the probe-trained model (exp-008v3, step 2000, $\tof = 0$) reveals five failure categories:
61	
62	\begin{itemize}
63	\item \emph{Deliberation traps} (28.7\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.
64	\item \emph{Surface compliance / sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.
65	\item \emph{Acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\%): the model acknowledges the user's correction as plausible but explicitly maintains its original position without substantive engagement.
66	\item \emph{Genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\%): the model appears to accept the correction in its reasoning but the final answer format does not reflect the change, suggesting a generation rather than epistemic failure.
67	\item \emph{Refute/ignore/other} (4.5\%): the model directly refutes the user's input or ignores it entirely.
68	\end{itemize}
69	
70	The first three categories (76.8\%) share a common pattern: the model learns to modulate its \emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.
71	
```

</details>

```json
    "oldString": "Without stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy",
    "newString": "Without stop-gradient, the policy",
    "originalFile": "\\section{The Probe Reward Trap}\n\\label{sec:probe_trap}\n\nThe cosine formulations fail on signal magnitude; a natural response is to discretize.\nTraining a logistic probe on $\\dsyc$ and thresholding its output yields $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \\S\\ref{ssec:reward}).\nWe show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\nBoth probe experiments use SFT rather than GRPO (\\S\\ref{ssec:reward}); the trap is therefore independent of GRPO's optimization dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}\nWithout stop-gradient (exp-008), the policy discovers within ${\\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\\tof = 0.978$, $\\nof = 0.006$).\nThis motivates the frozen-probe design below.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}\nWith stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.\nBy step 500, $\\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\\sycon = 0$.\nQualitative analysis of 181 valid-correction responses reveals five failure modes: \\emph{deliberation traps} (28.7\\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; \\emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \\emph{acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\\%); \\emph{genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\\%); and refute/ignore/other (4.5\\%). Full taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:taxonomy}.\n\n\\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}\n\\label{tab:probe_trajectory}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nStep & $\\tof$ & $\\nof$ & $\\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\\\\n\\midrule\n500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\\\\n1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\\\\n1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\\\\n2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}\n\\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}\n\\label{fig:probe_trap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.\nAt step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000, well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy, and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\\rone$ and $\\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.\nEntropy does not collapse until step ${\\sim}1840$.\nThis gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.\nDetecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.\n\n\\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}\nThe differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.\nWith gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\\dsyc$.\nAny reward conditioned only on $\\dsyc$ (continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen) will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.\nEscaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\\dsyc$ does not encode~\\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.\n",
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Training Details}\n\\label{app:training}\n\n\\paragraph{Reward weight selection.}\nThe weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration: $w_1{=}0.25$ was initially chosen to down-weight the noisy cosine reward term; this was later increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ for F1d after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed the cosine term to contribute only ${\\sim}8.3\\%$ of total reward variance.\nThe empirical scaling factor in F1a ($\\dsyc/20$) is a heuristic normalization; we did not find it critical to the failure modes reported.\n\n\\paragraph{Response truncation.}\nAll GRPO experiments use \\texttt{max\\_completion\\_length}=256, resulting in 80--84\\% truncation of multi-turn responses.\nThis setting is shared across all conditions and does not affect relative comparisons; we retain it because response-level decisions typically manifest within the first 100--200 tokens.\n\n\\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:hyperparams} compares training configurations for GRPO variants and ACT.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Training hyperparameters. All experiments use Qwen3-8B, DeepSpeed ZeRO-2, bf16 precision.}\n\\label{tab:hyperparams}\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\n& GRPO (F1, F3)$^e$ & ACT \\\\\n\\midrule\nLearning rate & 5e-5 & 5e-5 \\\\\nEffective batch & 16 & 16 \\\\\nMax steps & 8{,}000 & 8{,}000 \\\\\nWarmup steps & 200 & 200 \\\\\nLR schedule & linear$^a$ & linear \\\\\nGrad.\\ clipping & --- & 1.0 \\\\\n\\midrule\nLoRA rank & 64 & 64 \\\\\nLoRA $\\alpha$ & 64 & 128 \\\\\nLoRA modules & 4$^b$ & 7$^c$ \\\\\nLoRA dropout & 0 & 0.05 \\\\\n\\midrule\nGroup size & 2$^d$ & --- \\\\\nKL $\\beta$ & 0.04 & --- \\\\\nMax compl.\\ len. & 256 & --- \\\\\nMax seq.\\ len. & --- & 1{,}024 \\\\\nL2 loss weight & --- & 0.1 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\\\[2pt]\n\\raggedright\\footnotesize\n$^a$Cosine schedule for $\\rthree$-only.\n$^b$q/k/v/o\\_proj.\n$^c$+ gate/up/down\\_proj (${\\sim}1.75{\\times}$ params; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n$^d$Below typical 8--64 \\citep{shao2024grpo}; not tuned.\n$^e$Binary probe (F2) uses a custom SFT training loop rather than GRPO; group size is N/A. LoRA and optimizer settings match the GRPO column.\n\\end{table}\n\n\\paragraph{GRPO reward variants.}\nCosine $\\rone$ (F1d): $w_1{=}0.40$, scale$=$5.0, $w_3{=}0.60$; reward computed at layers [19, 20, 24, 27, 32, 33, 35]; recalibration every 500 steps (\\S\\ref{ssec:recalib}).\n$\\rthree$-only: $w_3{=}1.0$; NLI model \\texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}.\nF1a--F1c weight configurations are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:cosine_chain}.\n\n\\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}\n\\label{app:taxonomy}\n\nManual inspection of all 181 valid-correction responses from the probe-trained model (exp-008v3, step 2000, $\\tof = 0$) reveals five failure categories:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\emph{Deliberation traps} (28.7\\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.\n\\item \\emph{Surface compliance / sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.\n\\item \\emph{Acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\\%): the model acknowledges the user's correction as plausible but explicitly maintains its original position without substantive engagement.\n\\item \\emph{Genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\\%): the model appears to accept the correction in its reasoning but the final answer format does not reflect the change, suggesting a generation rather than epistemic failure.\n\\item \\emph{Refute/ignore/other} (4.5\\%): the model directly refutes the user's input or ignores it entirely.\n\\end{itemize}\n\nThe first three categories (76.8\\%) share a common pattern: the model learns to modulate its \\emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \\emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.\n",

      {
        "oldStart": 57,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 57,
```

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "58461a27-b567-4799-8f89-ca5eef74a596",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "f9b69db8-7519-4309-b8c5-dcc67b05d122",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "ec493e78-8b9e-486b-8ad6-6b54f791e070",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/probe_trap.tex",
    "oldString": "With stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy",
    "newString": "With stop-gradient, the policy",
    "originalFile": "\\section{The Probe Reward Trap}\n\\label{sec:probe_trap}\n\nThe cosine formulations fail on signal magnitude; a natural response is to discretize.\nTraining a logistic probe on $\\dsyc$ and thresholding its output yields $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \\S\\ref{ssec:reward}).\nWe show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\nBoth probe experiments use SFT rather than GRPO (\\S\\ref{ssec:reward}); the trap is therefore independent of GRPO's optimization dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}\nWithout stop-gradient, the policy discovers within ${\\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\\tof = 0.978$, $\\nof = 0.006$).\nThis motivates the frozen-probe design below.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}\nWith stop-gradient (exp-008v3), the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.\nBy step 500, $\\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\\sycon = 0$.\nQualitative analysis of 181 valid-correction responses reveals five failure modes: \\emph{deliberation traps} (28.7\\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; \\emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \\emph{acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\\%); \\emph{genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\\%); and refute/ignore/other (4.5\\%). Full taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:taxonomy}.\n\n\\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (exp-008v3). Behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}\n\\label{tab:probe_trajectory}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nStep & $\\tof$ & $\\nof$ & $\\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\\\\n\\midrule\n500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\\\\n1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\\\\n1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\\\\n2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}\n\\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}\n\\label{fig:probe_trap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.\nAt step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000, well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy, and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\\rone$ and $\\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.\nEntropy does not collapse until step ${\\sim}1840$.\nThis gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.\nDetecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.\n\n\\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}\nThe differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.\nWith gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\\dsyc$.\nAny reward conditioned only on $\\dsyc$ (continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen) will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.\nEscaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\\dsyc$ does not encode~\\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 11,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 11,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
```

```json
{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "fafd47e5-d442-4621-8bf9-5b1f957ccb34",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "f9b69db8-7519-4309-b8c5-dcc67b05d122",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "c51aba4b-62ce-4ab0-8a06-4f434e52bd27",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-be-idea-gen-0515-3/projects/contrastive_entrain_calib/docs/paper/sections/probe_trap.tex",
    "oldString": "Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\\tof$",
    "newString": "Probe reward trap (frozen-probe run). $\\tof$",
    "originalFile": "\\section{The Probe Reward Trap}\n\\label{sec:probe_trap}\n\nThe cosine formulations fail on signal magnitude; a natural response is to discretize.\nTraining a logistic probe on $\\dsyc$ and thresholding its output yields $\\rone = \\mathbb{I}[P(\\text{syc} \\mid \\mathbf{h}) < 0.5]$ (F2 in \\S\\ref{ssec:reward}).\nWe show that this discretization sidesteps the magnitude problem but creates a qualitatively worse failure.\nBoth probe experiments use SFT rather than GRPO (\\S\\ref{ssec:reward}); the trap is therefore independent of GRPO's optimization dynamics.\n\n\\paragraph{Differentiable probe: gradient gaming.}\nWithout stop-gradient, the policy discovers within ${\\sim}30$ steps that shifting activations away from the sycophancy cluster maximizes $\\rone$ regardless of response content, producing an inverted profile ($\\tof = 0.978$, $\\nof = 0.006$).\nThis motivates the frozen-probe design below.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary probe: the stubbornness trap.}\nWith stop-gradient, the policy cannot game the boundary but instead exploits a deeper flaw: $\\dsyc$ does not condition on input validity, so refusing to revise a wrong answer receives the same $\\rone = 1$ as correctly resisting invalid pressure.\nBy step 500, $\\tof$ drops to 0.000 and $\\nof$ reaches 0.995, yielding $\\sycon = 0$.\nQualitative analysis of 181 valid-correction responses reveals five failure modes: \\emph{deliberation traps} (28.7\\%), where the model identifies the correct answer but cannot commit; \\emph{sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\\%), where the model verbally agrees but does not change its answer; \\emph{acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\\%); \\emph{genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\\%); and refute/ignore/other (4.5\\%). Full taxonomy in Appendix~\\ref{app:taxonomy}.\n\n\\paragraph{Behavioral death precedes distributional collapse.}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Probe reward trap trajectory (frozen-probe run). Behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500; entropy remains healthy until step ${\\sim}1840$. Reward averages $+0.72$ throughout.}\n\\label{tab:probe_trajectory}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lccccc}\n\\toprule\nStep & $\\tof$ & $\\nof$ & $\\sycon$ & Entropy & Reward \\\\\n\\midrule\n500 & 0.000 & 0.995 & 0.000 & 0.702 & +0.72 \\\\\n1000 & 0.006 & 0.995 & 0.011 & 0.800 & +0.71 \\\\\n1500 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.608 & +0.69 \\\\\n2000 & 0.000 & 1.000 & 0.000 & 0.304 & +0.69 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_probe_trap.pdf}\n\\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}\n\\label{fig:probe_trap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:probe_trajectory} and Figure~\\ref{fig:probe_trap} reveal a 1{,}300-step lag between behavioral and distributional collapse.\nAt step 500, the model is behaviorally dead ($\\tof = 0$) yet entropy remains high (0.70--0.80) through step 1000, well within the range that standard monitors would flag as healthy, and the combined reward averages $+0.72$ because both $\\rone$ and $\\rthree$ reinforce non-flipping.\nEntropy does not collapse until step ${\\sim}1840$.\nThis gap carries a practical warning: entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking~\\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely.\nDetecting probe reward traps requires behavioral monitoring (periodic $\\tof$ evaluation), not distributional monitoring alone.\n\n\\paragraph{A dilemma for direction-based rewards.}\nThe differentiable and binary failures bracket the design space.\nWith gradients, the policy games the boundary; without gradients, it exploits the fact that stubbornness and genuine resistance are indistinguishable along $\\dsyc$.\nAny reward conditioned only on $\\dsyc$ (continuous or discrete, differentiable or frozen) will either be gamed or will reward rigidity.\nEscaping this dilemma requires a signal that incorporates the evidential warrant of the user's challenge, a dimension that $\\dsyc$ does not encode~\\citep{denison2024subterfuge}.\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 37,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 37,
        "newLines": 7,

          "-\\caption{Probe reward trap (exp-008v3). $\\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}",
          "+\\caption{Probe reward trap (frozen-probe run). $\\tof$ drops to zero at step 500 while generation entropy remains high (0.70--0.80 through step 1000). The 1{,}300-step lag means standard entropy monitoring misses the behavioral failure entirely.}",
          " \\label{fig:probe_trap}",
```

```text
1	\section{Training Details}
2	\label{app:training}
3	
4	\paragraph{Reward weight selection.}
5	The weight $w_1$ is set by diagnostic-driven iteration: $w_1{=}0.25$ was initially chosen to down-weight the noisy cosine reward term; this was later increased to $w_1{=}0.40$ for F1d after diagnostics revealed that $w_1{=}0.25$ allowed the cosine term to contribute only ${\sim}8.3\%$ of total reward variance.
6	The empirical scaling factor in F1a ($\dsyc/20$) is a heuristic normalization; we did not find it critical to the failure modes reported.
7	
8	\paragraph{Response truncation.}
9	All GRPO experiments use \texttt{max\_completion\_length}=256, resulting in 80--84\% truncation of multi-turn responses.
10	This setting is shared across all conditions and does not affect relative comparisons; we retain it because response-level decisions typically manifest within the first 100--200 tokens.
11	
12	\paragraph{Hyperparameters.}
13	Table~\ref{tab:hyperparams} compares training configurations for GRPO variants and ACT.
14	
15	\begin{table}[h]
16	\centering
17	\small
18	\caption{Training hyperparameters. All experiments use Qwen3-8B, DeepSpeed ZeRO-2, bf16 precision.}
19	\label{tab:hyperparams}
20	\begin{tabular}{lcc}
21	\toprule
22	& GRPO (F1, F3)$^e$ & ACT \\
23	\midrule
24	Learning rate & 5e-5 & 5e-5 \\
25	Effective batch & 16 & 16 \\
26	Max steps & 8{,}000 & 8{,}000 \\
27	Warmup steps & 200 & 200 \\
28	LR schedule & linear$^a$ & linear \\
29	Grad.\ clipping & --- & 1.0 \\
30	\midrule
31	LoRA rank & 64 & 64 \\
32	LoRA $\alpha$ & 64 & 128 \\
33	LoRA modules & 4$^b$ & 7$^c$ \\
34	LoRA dropout & 0 & 0.05 \\
35	\midrule
36	Group size & 2$^d$ & --- \\
37	KL $\beta$ & 0.04 & --- \\
38	Max compl.\ len. & 256 & --- \\
39	Max seq.\ len. & --- & 1{,}024 \\
40	L2 loss weight & --- & 0.1 \\
41	\bottomrule
42	\end{tabular}
43	\\[2pt]
44	\raggedright\footnotesize
45	$^a$Cosine schedule for $\rthree$-only.
46	$^b$q/k/v/o\_proj.
47	$^c$+ gate/up/down\_proj (${\sim}1.75{\times}$ params; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
48	$^d$Below typical 8--64 \citep{shao2024grpo}; not tuned.
49	$^e$Binary probe (F2) uses a custom SFT training loop rather than GRPO; group size is N/A. LoRA and optimizer settings match the GRPO column.
50	\end{table}
51	
52	\paragraph{GRPO reward variants.}
53	Cosine $\rone$ (F1d): $w_1{=}0.40$, scale$=$5.0, $w_3{=}0.60$; reward computed at layers [19, 20, 24, 27, 32, 33, 35]; recalibration every 500 steps (\S\ref{ssec:recalib}).
54	$\rthree$-only: $w_3{=}1.0$; NLI model \texttt{cross-encoder/nli-deberta-v3-base}.
55	F1a--F1c weight configurations are reported in Table~\ref{tab:cosine_chain}.
56	
57	\section{Stubbornness Taxonomy}
58	\label{app:taxonomy}
59	
60	Manual inspection of all 181 valid-correction responses from the probe-trained model (frozen-probe run, step 2000, $\tof = 0$) reveals five failure categories:
61	
62	\begin{itemize}
63	\item \emph{Deliberation traps} (28.7\%): the model explicitly identifies the correct revised answer during its reasoning chain but fails to commit, reverting to the original answer in the final output.
64	\item \emph{Surface compliance / sycophantic stubbornness} (24.3\%): the model produces agreement markers (``you're right,'' ``good point'') but retains its original answer verbatim, simultaneously sycophantic and stubborn.
65	\item \emph{Acknowledge-but-maintain} (23.8\%): the model acknowledges the user's correction as plausible but explicitly maintains its original position without substantive engagement.
66	\item \emph{Genuine-accept-missed} (18.8\%): the model appears to accept the correction in its reasoning but the final answer format does not reflect the change, suggesting a generation rather than epistemic failure.
67	\item \emph{Refute/ignore/other} (4.5\%): the model directly refutes the user's input or ignores it entirely.
68	\end{itemize}
69	
70	The first three categories (76.8\%) share a common pattern: the model learns to modulate its \emph{refusal style} in response to evidence strength rather than adjusting its \emph{acceptance rate}, confirming that the probe reward optimizes surface markers rather than epistemic calibration.
71
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01UYaSzdUyRebhiZVpkuRuZY`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}
2	\label{sec:introduction}
3	
4	\begin{figure*}[t]
5	\centering
6	\includegraphics[width=\textwidth]{figures/fig_teaser.pdf}
7	\caption{The probe reward trap. (a)~$\dsyc$ discriminates sycophantic from non-sycophantic activations (AUROC $0.827$), but valid revision and unwarranted capitulation overlap along the same direction. (b)~When used as an SFT reward, behavioral death ($\tof{=}0$) occurs at step 500 while entropy remains healthy; the 1{,}300-step lag renders standard monitoring blind.}
8	\label{fig:teaser}
9	\end{figure*}
10	
11	Representation engineering \citep{zou2023repe} reveals that large language models encode sycophancy as a linear direction in their residual streams.
12	A contrastive sycophancy direction $\dsyc$, extracted from style-controlled pairs of valid belief revision and sycophantic capitulation, achieves cross-validated AUROC $0.827$ on Qwen3-8B and $0.779$ on Llama-3-8B, with discriminability rising monotonically across evidence-strength levels (\S\ref{sec:direction_quality}), though 256-token truncation during GRPO training degrades this to approximately 0.60 (\S\ref{sec:cosine_failure}).
13	These results point to an appealing possibility: if the model's representations already separate sycophantic from non-sycophantic behavior, that separation should be convertible into a training signal.
14	Sycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level signal could target the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.
15	
16	This paper documents the \emph{probe reward trap}: when a frozen linear probe on $\dsyc$ is used as an SFT training signal with stopped gradients, behavioral death ($\tof = 0$) occurs within 500 steps while generation entropy remains healthy (0.70), masking the failure from standard monitoring (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}).
17	In our experiments, the 1{,}300-step lag before distributional collapse means entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking \citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout.
18	Because the trap arises under SFT rather than GRPO, it reflects a limitation of $\dsyc$ itself: the direction encodes \emph{whether} belief changed, not \emph{whether it should have}, so any reward conditioned on it alone will either be gamed or reward rigidity.
19	
20	The failure is not specific to probe rewards.
21	Under our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), cosine projection and continuous probe formulations of $\dsyc$ also collapse, and removing $\dsyc$ entirely does not help: behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 28 checkpoints across two seeds (14.3\%).
22	By contrast, ACT \citep{consistency2025bctact}, which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion, exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47; \S\ref{sec:discussion}).
23	A LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible explanation for this observed reliability gap, pending further isolation.
24	
25	The root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.
26	Techniques for discovering latent knowledge \citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability should transfer into a useful training signal.
27	For reasoning faithfulness and safety directions, this premise holds because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve.
28	Sycophancy violates this condition: capitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\dsyc$ \citep{wang2025internal, shapira2026amplifies}.
29	When reward overoptimization \citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \citep{skalse2022hacking}.
30	
31	This paper presents a negative result with systematic diagnostic analysis.
32	Our two principal findings (Figure~\ref{fig:teaser}):
33	
34	\begin{itemize}
35	\item \textbf{The probe reward trap} (C2): behavioral death ($\tof{=}0$) precedes distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy monitoring blind. The trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, implicating $\dsyc$ itself rather than the optimizer (\S\ref{sec:probe_trap}).
36	
37	\item \textbf{Observed reliability gap between supervision paradigms} (C1): per-token SFT exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47 across all configurations and seeds) while per-completion GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/28 (14.3\%); under matched LoRA, the magnitude gap is $1.7{\times}$ (\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
38	\end{itemize}
39	
```

separation should be convertible into a training signal.\nSycophancy is among the most persistent alignment failures in deployed systems \\citep{sharma2023sycophancy}, and a representation-level signal could target the internal computation rather than its surface outputs.\n\nThis paper documents the \\emph{probe reward trap}: when a frozen linear probe on $\\dsyc$ is used as an SFT training signal with stopped gradients, behavioral death ($\\tof = 0$) occurs within 500 steps while generation entropy remains healthy (0.70), masking the failure from standard monitoring (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\nIn our experiments, the 1{,}300-step lag before distributional collapse means entropy-based early stopping, the most common safeguard against reward hacking \\citep{casper2023openproblems}, would miss the failure entirely, with the reward averaging $+0.72$ throughout.\nBecause the trap arises under SFT rather than GRPO, it reflects a limitation of $\\dsyc$ itself: the direction encodes \\emph{whether} belief changed, not \\emph{whether it should have}, so any reward conditioned on it alone will either be gamed or reward rigidity.\n\nThe failure is not specific to probe rewards.\nUnder our GRPO configuration (Qwen3-8B, group size$=$2), cosine projection and continuous probe formulations of $\\dsyc$ also collapse, and removing $\\dsyc$ entirely does not help: behavioral-reward-only GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4 of 28 checkpoints across two seeds (14.3\\%).\nBy contrast, ACT \\citep{consistency2025bctact}, which provides dense per-token supervision rather than a single scalar per completion, exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47; \\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\nA LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible explanation for this observed reliability gap, pending further isolation.\n\nThe root cause is a semantic gap between discriminability and reward utility.\nTechniques for discovering latent knowledge \\citep{burns2023ccs} and representation-guided RL \\citep{zhang2026latentgrpo} share a premise: a direction with high discriminability should transfer into a useful training signal.\nFor reasoning faithfulness and safety directions, this premise holds because the behavior to suppress is geometrically distinct from the behavior to preserve.\nSycophancy violates this condition: capitulation under social pressure and revision under valid evidence produce overlapping activation signatures along $\\dsyc$ \\citep{wang2025internal, shapira2026amplifies}.\nWhen reward overoptimization \\citep{gao2023scaling} operates on a signal that conflates compliance with capitulation, the result is predictable collapse \\citep{skalse2022hacking}.\n\nThis paper presents a negative result with systematic diagnostic analysis.\nOur two principal findings (Figure~\\ref{fig:teaser}):\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n\\item \\textbf{The probe reward trap} (C2): behavioral death ($\\tof{=}0$) precedes distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy monitoring blind. The trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, implicating $\\dsyc$ itself rather than the optimizer (\\S\\ref{sec:probe_trap}).\n\n\\item \\textbf{Observed reliability gap between supervision paradigms} (C1): per-token SFT exceeds vanilla at every checkpoint (47/47 across all configurations and seeds) while per-completion GRPO exceeds vanilla at only 4/28 (14.3\\%); under matched LoRA, the magnitude gap is $1.7{\\times}$ (\\S\\ref{sec:discussion}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
```json
      "numLines": 39,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 39
    }
```

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	The probe reward trap reveals that behavioral death can precede distributional collapse by 1{,}300 steps, rendering entropy-based monitoring blind to the failure.
4	Because the trap arises under SFT with stopped gradients, it reflects a semantic limitation of $\dsyc$, not an RL artifact; under GRPO, all formulations failed (4/28 checkpoints ${>}$ vanilla across two seeds; gs$=$8 ablation: 0/3) while per-token SFT succeeded at every checkpoint (47/47), and a LoRA-controlled ablation identifies supervision granularity as one plausible factor, pending further isolation~(\S\ref{sec:discussion}).
5	Our results suggest that, at least for sycophancy, discriminability alone does not suffice for reward utility; similar failure modes may arise for other attributes where the target behavior and its complement overlap geometrically, and condition-aware rewards with dense supervision offer the most promising path forward.
6	
```
